# Unprimed answers compared: Haiku, Sonnet

Each row is one prompt from `haiku-unprimed/prompts.json`, sent once to claude-haiku-5-5 and claude-sonnet-5-5 on 2026-10-15. The prompts describe what the reader should see and never name a syntax, a format or a lineage. The feature names follow `feature-union.md`.

Context isolation: a neutral empty working directory, the system prompt replaced with "You are a helpful assistant.", no tools, no MCP servers, no setting sources, and CLAUDE.md files excluded. A canary check found only Claude Code's environment block (working directory, platform, model ID, date) and the account email in context. That block is itself laid out with a Markdown heading and bullets, a mild nudge toward Markdown that could not be removed, and the email let the model infer the user's name.

Single samples, no temperature control. Answers are shown exactly as returned; raw JSON is in `haiku-unprimed/raw*/`. Rerun with `python3 haiku-unprimed/run.py [--model sonnet]` and rebuild with `python3 haiku-unprimed/build_table.py`.

| # | Feature | Prompt | Haiku | Sonnet |
|---|---|---|---|---|

&#42;&#42;Breaks&#58;&#42;&#42; Step away from the screen at least once an hour to stretch and reset&#46;<br>&#45; &#42;&#42;Boundaries&#58;&#42;&#42; When the workday ends, close your work apps and log off to protect your time off&#46;<br>&#96;&#96;&#96;<br><br>To generate the table of contents automatically, render the file with a tool that supports the &#96;&#91;TOC&#93;&#96; marker, such as Python&#45;Markdown, Typora, or Obsidian&#46; With Pandoc, use &#96;pandoc &#45;&#45;toc &#45;s guide&#46;md &#45;o guide&#46;html&#96; instead and remove the &#96;&#91;TOC&#93;&#96; line&#46;</code> | <code>Here is a short LaTeX document whose table of contents is generated automatically from the section headings&#58;<br><br>&#96;&#96;&#96;latex<br>&#92;documentclass&#123;article&#125;<br><br>&#92;title&#123;A Short Document&#125;<br>&#92;author&#123;Joseph Wecker&#125;<br>&#92;date&#123;&#92;today&#125;<br><br>&#92;begin&#123;document&#125;<br><br>&#92;maketitle<br>&#92;tableofcontents<br>&#92;newpage<br><br>&#92;section&#123;Introduction&#125;<br>This document demonstrates a simple structure with three sections&#46;<br>The table of contents above is built automatically from the headings&#46;<br><br>&#92;section&#123;Main Content&#125;<br>This section holds the core material&#46; Add paragraphs, lists, or<br>figures here, and the table of contents will update on its own&#46;<br><br>&#92;section&#123;Conclusion&#125;<br>Three sections, one automatically generated table of contents,<br>and no manual numbering to maintain&#46;<br><br>&#92;end&#123;document&#125;<br>&#96;&#96;&#96;<br><br>&#42;&#42;To build it&#58;&#42;&#42; save it as &#96;doc&#46;tex&#96; and run &#96;pdflatex doc&#46;tex&#96; &#42;&#42;twice&#42;&#42;&#46; The first pass collects the headings and the second fills in the table of contents&#46;<br><br>&#42;&#42;Markdown alternative&#58;&#42;&#42; if you&#39;d rather use Markdown, write the three sections as &#96;&#35;&#96; headings in &#96;doc&#46;md&#96; and generate the table of contents with Pandoc&#58;<br><br>&#96;&#96;&#96;sh<br>pandoc doc&#46;md &#45;&#45;toc &#45;s &#45;o doc&#46;pdf<br>&#96;&#96;&#96;<br><br>I can also write either version to a file in your working directory if you&#39;d like&#46;</code> |
| 2.5 | Thematic break | Write two short paragraphs separated by a horizontal divider line. | <code>The best time to start a habit is usually today, not some imagined future day when your schedule is calmer&#46; Small, specific actions work better than broad resolutions&#46; Instead of promising to &#34;exercise more,&#34; commit to a ten&#45;minute walk after lunch&#46; Once that becomes automatic, you can build on it, and consistency matters far more than intensity in the early weeks&#46;<br><br>&#45;&#45;&#45;<br><br>Progress also depends on how you measure it&#46; Tracking a single habit on a calendar, with a simple mark for each day you complete it, makes the effort visible and gives you a reason to keep the streak going&#46; Missing a day is not a failure, but skipping two in a row is worth noticing&#46; Treat each lapse as information about what got in the way, and adjust the plan so it&#39;s easier to repeat the next day&#46;</code> | <code>The morning light spilled across the kitchen table, catching the steam that curled upward from a fresh cup of coffee&#46; Outside, the city was just beginning to stir, with distant footsteps and the low hum of traffic building into the familiar rhythm of a new day&#46;<br><br>&#45;&#45;&#45;<br><br>By evening, the same table had transformed into a quiet space for reflection&#46; The coffee cup was replaced by a book and a lamp&#39;s warm glow, and the day&#39;s noise gave way to a calm stillness that made everything feel unhurried and complete&#46;</code> |
| 2.6 | Page break | Write two short paragraphs with a page break between them for printing. | <code>Paragraph one goes here&#46; Write it as a complete, self&#45;contained passage that opens the document and sets up the subject, so a reader on the first printed page gets the full context&#46;<br><br>&#60;div style&#61;&#34;page&#45;break&#45;after&#58; always;&#34;&#62;&#60;/div&#62;<br><br>Paragraph two goes here&#46; It picks up where the first paragraph left off and carries the subject to a conclusion, so the two pages together read as one coherent piece&#46;</code> | <code>The morning light settled slowly over the harbor, turning the water from slate gray to a soft, shimmering gold&#46; Fishing boats rocked gently at their moorings, and the first gulls wheeled overhead, calling out to a town that was only beginning to wake&#46; Somewhere in the distance, a bakery door swung open, releasing the warm smell of fresh bread into the cool air&#46;<br><br>&#60;div style&#61;&#34;page&#45;break&#45;after&#58; always;&#34;&#62;&#60;/div&#62;<br><br>By afternoon, the harbor had settled into a lazy rhythm&#46; Shopkeepers propped their doors open, children darted between the market stalls, and the sun climbed high enough to warm the old stone piers&#46; It was the kind of day that asked nothing of anyone, and the whole town seemed content to simply let it unfold&#46;<br><br>The &#96;&#60;div&#62;&#96; between the paragraphs forces a page break when this is printed from a browser or a Markdown&#45;to&#45;PDF tool&#46; If you paste it into a word processor instead, insert a manual page break &#40;Ctrl&#43;Enter, or Cmd&#43;Enter on Mac&#41; in its place&#46;</code> |
| 2.7 | Parts and chapters | Write the opening structure of a short book with two parts, each containing two chapters, with one sentence of text in each chapter. | <code>&#42;&#42;Part One&#58; The Departure&#42;&#42;<br><br>&#42;&#42;Chapter 1&#58; The Letter&#42;&#42;<br>The envelope arrived on a rainy Tuesday, bearing a seal no one in the village had seen before&#46;<br><br>&#42;&#42;Chapter 2&#58; The Road North&#42;&#42;<br>She packed only what she could carry, and she left before the sun had cleared the hills&#46;<br><br>&#42;&#42;Part Two&#58; The Crossing&#42;&#42;<br><br>&#42;&#42;Chapter 3&#58; The River&#42;&#42;<br>The ferryman refused her coins and asked only for the name she had been hiding since childhood&#46;<br><br>&#42;&#42;Chapter 4&#58; The Stone Gate&#42;&#42;<br>Beyond the gate, the path split in two, and only one of the roads would lead her home&#46;</code> | <code>&#35; The Long Way Home<br><br>&#35;&#35; Part One&#58; Departure<br><br>&#35;&#35;&#35; Chapter 1&#58; The Letter<br><br>The envelope arrived on a gray Tuesday, bearing no return address and a handwriting Mara had not seen in twenty years&#46;<br><br>&#35;&#35;&#35; Chapter 2&#58; The Packing<br><br>By nightfall she had filled a single suitcase, leaving behind everything except the letter and the key that had fallen out of it&#46;<br><br>&#45;&#45;&#45;<br><br>&#35;&#35; Part Two&#58; Arrival<br><br>&#35;&#35;&#35; Chapter 3&#58; The Station<br><br>The train let her off at a platform so small it had no name, only a rusted lamp swinging in the wind&#46;<br><br>&#35;&#35;&#35; Chapter 4&#58; The House<br><br>At the end of the lane stood the house from her childhood, and the key turned in the lock as though it had been waiting for her all along&#46;</code> |
| 3.1 | Hard line breaks | Write a mailing address for a fictional person, keeping each part of the address on its own line. | <code>Jordan Ellis<br>1482 Maple Hollow Drive<br>Apt 3B<br>Springfield, OR 97477<br>United States</code> | <code>Eleanor M&#46; Whitcombe<br>4721 Larkspur Hollow Lane<br>Apartment 3B<br>Maplewood, OR 97204<br>United States</code> |
| 3.2 | Line blocks (verse) | Write a four-line poem where the second and fourth lines are indented, and the line breaks and indentation must be preserved exactly when displayed. | <code>&#96;&#96;&#96;<br>The morning light spills over the hill,<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;and birds begin their quiet song&#46;<br>The river wakes and hums along,<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;as day unfolds, serene and still&#46;<br>&#96;&#96;&#96;</code> | <code>&#96;&#96;&#96;<br>The morning light spills over the hill,<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;soft gold on the sleeping town;<br>The river hums, calm and still,<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;as the night folds its shadows down&#46;<br>&#96;&#96;&#96;</code> |
| 3.3 | Non-breaking space | Write a sentence mentioning Dr. Smith, where 'Dr.' and 'Smith' must never be split across two lines. | <code>The appointment was confirmed by Dr&#46;&#38;nbsp;Smith this morning&#46;<br><br>The sentence uses a non&#45;breaking space &#40;&#96;&#38;nbsp;&#96; in HTML, U&#43;00A0 in plain text&#41; between &#34;Dr&#46;&#34; and &#34;Smith,&#34; so line&#45;wrapping can&#39;t separate them&#46;</code> | <code>Here&#39;s a sentence using a non&#45;breaking space &#40;U&#43;00A0&#41; between &#34;Dr&#46;&#34; and &#34;Smith&#34; so they stay together on one line&#58;<br><br>The committee asked Dr&#46; Smith to present the findings at the annual conference&#46;<br><br>The space between &#34;Dr&#46;&#34; and &#34;Smith&#34; is a non&#45;breaking space, which prevents a line wrap from occurring there&#46;<br><br>For other contexts&#58;<br><br>&#45; &#42;&#42;HTML&#58;&#42;&#42; &#96;Dr&#46;&#38;nbsp;Smith&#96;<br>&#45; &#42;&#42;LaTeX&#58;&#42;&#42; &#96;Dr&#46;&#126;Smith&#96;<br>&#45; &#42;&#42;Markdown/plain text&#58;&#42;&#42; use the literal U&#43;00A0 character</code> |

| 3.5 | Smart dashes and ellipsis | Write a sentence with the date range 1990 to 2000, an aside set off with a long dash, and a trailing ellipsis, all displayed with proper typographic characters. | <code>The software industry expanded rapidly during 1990–2000 — a decade of quiet ambition and louder promises — and few could have guessed where it would lead…</code> | <code>The years 1990–2000 saw the internet grow from a niche academic tool—used by a few thousand researchers—into a global force that reshaped how people lived, worked, and talked to one another…<br><br>Typographic characters used&#58;<br>&#45; &#42;&#42;En dash &#40;–&#41;&#42;&#42; in the date range 1990–2000<br>&#45; &#42;&#42;Em dashes &#40;—&#41;&#42;&#42; setting off the aside<br>&#45; &#42;&#42;Horizontal ellipsis &#40;…&#41;&#42;&#42; as a single character at the end</code> |

| 4.5 | Highlight with color | Write the sentence 'Pay rent and call mom' with 'Pay rent' highlighted in red and 'call mom' highlighted in green. | <code>Here&#39;s the sentence with colored highlighting as HTML&#58;<br><br>&#60;span style&#61;&#34;background&#45;color&#58; red; color&#58; white;&#34;&#62;Pay rent&#60;/span&#62; and &#60;span style&#61;&#34;background&#45;color&#58; green; color&#58; white;&#34;&#62;call mom&#60;/span&#62;<br><br>If your display doesn&#39;t render HTML, I can give you a version using ANSI terminal color codes instead&#46;</code> | <code>Here&#39;s the sentence with the highlights, as HTML&#58;<br><br>&#96;&#96;&#96;html<br>&#60;p&#62;&#60;mark style&#61;&#34;background&#45;color&#58; red; color&#58; white;&#34;&#62;Pay rent&#60;/mark&#62; and &#60;mark style&#61;&#34;background&#45;color&#58; green; color&#58; white;&#34;&#62;call mom&#60;/mark&#62;&#60;/p&#62;<br>&#96;&#96;&#96;<br><br>Rendered, it looks like this&#58; &#60;span style&#61;&#34;background&#45;color&#58;red;color&#58;white;&#34;&#62;Pay rent&#60;/span&#62; and &#60;span style&#61;&#34;background&#45;color&#58;green;color&#58;white;&#34;&#62;call mom&#60;/span&#62;<br><br>If you want it in a terminal, use ANSI escape codes&#58;<br><br>&#96;&#96;&#96;bash<br>printf &#39;&#92;e&#91;41mPay rent&#92;e&#91;0m and &#92;e&#91;42mcall mom&#92;e&#91;0m&#92;n&#39;<br>&#96;&#96;&#96;<br><br>Plain Markdown has no color support, so which format works best depends on where you plan to use it&#46;</code> |

| 4.11 | Inline math | Write a sentence that includes Einstein's mass-energy equation as a properly typeset formula. | <code>Einstein&#39;s mass&#45;energy equivalence states that &#36;E &#61; mc&#94;2&#36;, where &#36;E&#36; is energy, &#36;m&#36; is mass, and &#36;c&#36; is the speed of light in a vacuum&#46;</code> | <code>Einstein&#39;s famous insight that mass and energy are interchangeable is captured in the equation &#36;E &#61; mc&#94;2&#36;, where &#36;E&#36; is energy, &#36;m&#36; is mass, and &#36;c&#36; is the speed of light in a vacuum&#46;<br><br>In display form&#58;<br><br>&#36;&#36;E &#61; mc&#94;2&#36;&#36;</code> |
| 4.12 | Abbreviations | Write a sentence using the abbreviation HTML, defined so that its full form appears when the reader hovers over it. | <code>Here is the sentence, marked up with an &#96;&#60;abbr&#62;&#96; element&#46; The &#96;title&#96; attribute holds the full form, which most browsers show as a tooltip on hover&#58;<br><br>&#96;&#96;&#96;html<br>&#60;p&#62;Most modern websites are built with &#60;abbr title&#61;&#34;HyperText Markup Language&#34;&#62;HTML&#60;/abbr&#62;, which structures their content&#46;&#60;/p&#62;<br>&#96;&#96;&#96;</code> | <code>Use the &#96;&#60;abbr&#62;&#96; element with a &#96;title&#96; attribute&#58;<br><br>&#96;&#96;&#96;html<br>&#60;p&#62;Most web pages are built with &#60;abbr title&#61;&#34;HyperText Markup Language&#34;&#62;HTML&#60;/abbr&#62;, which defines their structure&#46;&#60;/p&#62;<br>&#96;&#96;&#96;<br><br>When the reader hovers over &#34;HTML,&#34; the browser displays a tooltip reading &#34;HyperText Markup Language&#46;&#34;</code> |
| 4.13 | Index terms | Write a sentence about photosynthesis, marking the word 'photosynthesis' as an entry for the book's index. | <code>Plants convert sunlight, water, and carbon dioxide into glucose and oxygen through &#92;index&#123;photosynthesis&#125;photosynthesis&#92;index&#123;photosynthesis&#125;&#46;<br><br>This uses LaTeX&#39;s &#96;&#92;index&#123;&#125;&#96; command&#46; If your book uses a different indexing system, tell me which one and I&#39;ll adjust the markup&#46;</code> | <code>Here&#39;s a sentence with the index entry marked in LaTeX style&#58;<br><br>Plants convert sunlight, water, and carbon dioxide into glucose and oxygen through &#92;index&#123;photosynthesis&#125;photosynthesis, the process that underpins nearly all life on Earth&#46;<br><br>If you&#39;re using a different system, here are equivalents&#58;<br><br>&#45; &#42;&#42;Markdown with Pandoc/other indexers &#40;HTML anchor&#41;&#58;&#42;&#42; &#96;&#60;span class&#61;&#34;index&#34; data&#45;index&#61;&#34;photosynthesis&#34;&#62;photosynthesis&#60;/span&#62;&#96;<br>&#45; &#42;&#42;Microsoft Word field code&#58;&#42;&#42; &#96;&#123; XE &#34;photosynthesis&#34; &#125;&#96; placed right after the word<br>&#45; &#42;&#42;DocBook/XML&#58;&#42;&#42; &#96;&#60;indexterm&#62;&#60;primary&#62;photosynthesis&#60;/primary&#62;&#60;/indexterm&#62;photosynthesis&#96;<br><br>Let me know which format your book uses and I can tailor it&#46;</code> |
| 4.14 | Generic inline span with attributes | Write a sentence where the phrase 'limited offer' is tagged with the class name 'promo' so a stylesheet can style it. | <code>Here&#39;s a sentence with &#34;limited offer&#34; wrapped in a &#96;span&#96; tagged with the &#96;promo&#96; class&#58;<br><br>&#96;&#96;&#96;html<br>&#60;p&#62;Act now, this is a &#60;span class&#61;&#34;promo&#34;&#62;limited offer&#60;/span&#62; you won&#39;t want to miss&#46;&#60;/p&#62;<br>&#96;&#96;&#96;<br><br>A stylesheet can then target it with &#96;&#46;promo &#123; &#46;&#46;&#46; &#125;&#96; or &#96;span&#46;promo &#123; &#46;&#46;&#46; &#125;&#96;&#46;</code> | <code>&#96;&#96;&#96;html<br>&#60;p&#62;Don&#39;t miss out on our &#60;span class&#61;&#34;promo&#34;&#62;limited offer&#60;/span&#62; this weekend only&#33;&#60;/p&#62;<br>&#96;&#96;&#96;<br><br>And a matching style rule&#58;<br><br>&#96;&#96;&#96;css<br>&#46;promo &#123;<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;color&#58; &#35;c0392b;<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;font&#45;weight&#58; bold;<br>&#125;<br>&#96;&#96;&#96;</code> |
| 4.15 | Spoiler | Write a sentence about a mystery novel where the name of the murderer is hidden until the reader clicks on it. | <code>The detective closes the case in the final chapter, but the killer&#39;s name stays hidden behind a click&#45;to&#45;reveal spoiler tag&#58; &#42;&#42;&#91;Click to reveal&#58; Eleanor Vance, the victim&#39;s trusted housekeeper&#93;&#42;&#42;&#46;</code> | <code>In this interactive mystery novel, the killer&#39;s identity stays concealed behind a blurred spoiler tag until you click &#42;&#42;&#91;REVEAL&#58; ████████&#93;&#42;&#42;, which only then unveils that it was the butler all along&#46;<br><br>If you&#39;d like, I can write it as actual HTML so the name is truly hidden until clicked&#58;<br><br>&#96;&#96;&#96;html<br>&#60;p&#62;<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;In this mystery novel, the murderer is<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&#60;span onclick&#61;&#34;this&#46;textContent&#61;&#39;the butler&#39;&#34; style&#61;&#34;cursor&#58;pointer; background&#58;&#35;000; color&#58;&#35;000;&#34;&#62;<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;the butler<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&#60;/span&#62;&#46;<br>&#60;/p&#62;<br>&#96;&#96;&#96;</code> |

| 7.4 | Sidebar | Write a short main paragraph and a sidebar with a related aside. | <code>&#42;&#42;Why Bread Rises&#42;&#42;<br><br>Bread rises because yeast consumes the sugars in flour and releases carbon dioxide gas&#46; As the dough kneads and proofs, that gas becomes trapped in a web of gluten, the stretchy protein network that forms when wheat flour is mixed with water&#46; The bubbles expand with heat in the oven until the crust sets, fixing the loaf&#39;s airy structure in place&#46;<br><br>&#62; &#42;&#42;Sidebar&#58; Did You Know?&#42;&#42;<br>&#62; Sourdough starters rely on wild yeast and bacteria rather than commercial yeast&#46; Their sour flavor comes from the lactic and acetic acids the bacteria produce, which is why a well&#45;kept starter can stay active for decades&#46;</code> | <code>&#42;&#42;Main Paragraph&#42;&#42;<br><br>Good writing begins with a clear sense of who it&#39;s for&#46; Before choosing a single word, a writer benefits from picturing one specific reader&#58; what they already know, what they want, and what they&#39;re likely to skim past&#46; That picture shapes everything that follows, from which details earn a place on the page to how quickly the main point arrives&#46; A paragraph written for &#34;everyone&#34; tends to satisfy no one, while one written for a particular person often ends up useful to many&#46;<br><br>&#62; &#42;&#42;Sidebar&#58; The Rubber Duck Principle&#42;&#42;<br>&#62;<br>&#62; Programmers have long used a quirky trick called &#34;rubber duck debugging&#34;&#58; explaining a problem aloud to an inanimate duck&#46; The act of putting thoughts into plain words for a patient, imaginary listener often reveals the flaw in the logic&#46; Writers can borrow the idea&#46; If you can&#39;t explain your main point to the duck in a sentence or two, the paragraph probably isn&#39;t ready yet&#46;</code> |

| 7.9 | Block alignment | Write a short title line and a paragraph, with the title line centered. | <code>&#60;p align&#61;&#34;center&#34;&#62;&#60;strong&#62;The Quiet Power of Small Habits&#60;/strong&#62;&#60;/p&#62;<br><br>Small habits often look insignificant on any given day, yet their effects compound over time&#46; A few minutes of reading, a short walk, or a simple daily plan can shape who we become far more than occasional bursts of effort&#46; The key is consistency rather than intensity&#58; when a habit is easy enough to repeat, it becomes part of our routine, and the progress that follows is steady and lasting&#46;</code> | <code>&#60;div align&#61;&#34;center&#34;&#62;<br><br>&#35; The Quiet Hour Before Dawn<br><br>&#60;/div&#62;<br><br>There is a particular stillness that settles over the world in the hour before sunrise, when the streets are empty, the air is cool and unhurried, and the first pale light begins to seep along the horizon&#46; It is a time that belongs to no one in particular, neither the end of the night nor quite the start of the day, and in that gap the mind feels unusually clear&#46; Thoughts that were tangled the evening before loosen and sort themselves, and small sounds, like a distant bird or the hum of a kettle, take on a gentle significance&#46; Those who wake early enough to notice it often find that the hour offers a kind of unspoken promise&#58; that whatever the day brings, it begins in calm&#46;</code> |

| 8.4 | Example lists | Write a passage with two numbered examples whose numbering continues across the whole document, and a later sentence that refers back to the second example by its number. | <code>Rounding rules can produce surprising results when values are summed&#46; Consider these two cases&#46;<br><br>&#42;&#42;Example 1&#46;&#42;&#42; A shop sells three items priced at &#36;0&#46;10, &#36;0&#46;10, and &#36;0&#46;10&#46; Each total is computed exactly, so the sum is &#36;0&#46;30, as expected&#46;<br><br>&#42;&#42;Example 2&#46;&#42;&#42; The same shop sells three items priced at &#36;0&#46;105 each, and it rounds each price to the nearest cent before adding them&#46; Each price rounds to &#36;0&#46;11, so the receipt totals &#36;0&#46;33&#46; The exact sum would be &#36;0&#46;315, which rounds to &#36;0&#46;32, so rounding each item first costs the customer an extra cent&#46;<br><br>Example 2 shows why accounting systems usually round only the final total, not each line item&#46;</code> | <code>&#35; Ambiguity in Everyday Language<br><br>Ambiguity is not always a flaw in communication&#46; Often it goes unnoticed because context quietly resolves it&#46; Consider a sentence whose structure allows two readings&#58;<br><br>&#42;&#42;&#40;1&#41;&#42;&#42; The teacher saw the student with the telescope&#46;<br><br>Here the phrase &#34;with the telescope&#34; could describe how the teacher saw the student, or it could identify which student was seen&#46; Most listeners pick one reading instantly and never notice the other&#46;<br><br>Ambiguity can also arise from a single word rather than from sentence structure&#58;<br><br>&#42;&#42;&#40;2&#41;&#42;&#42; She walked to the bank after the storm&#46;<br><br>The word &#34;bank&#34; might refer to a financial institution or to the edge of a river, and the mention of a storm nudges the reader toward the second meaning without ruling out the first&#46;<br><br>The difference between the two cases is worth noting&#46; In &#40;1&#41;, the ambiguity lives in the grammar, so rewording the sentence is the only way to resolve it&#46; In &#40;2&#41;, the ambiguity lives in the vocabulary, and a bit of added context, such as the word &#34;river,&#34; would settle it immediately&#46; This is why example &#40;2&#41; feels easier to repair than example &#40;1&#41;, even though both leave the reader with two possible interpretations&#46;</code> |

| 8.12 | Several blocks in one list item | Write a numbered list of two items where the first item contains two paragraphs and a short code example. | <code>1&#46; A Python decorator is a function that takes another function as its argument and returns a new function, usually one that wraps the original with extra behavior&#46; Instead of copying timing or logging code into each one, you define the logic once and apply it wherever it is needed&#46; Here is a short example that measures how long a function takes to run&#58;<br><br>&#96;&#96;&#96;python<br>import time<br>from functools import wraps<br><br>def timed&#40;func&#41;&#58;<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&#64;wraps&#40;func&#41;<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;def wrapper&#40;&#42;args, &#42;&#42;kwargs&#41;&#58;<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;start &#61; time&#46;perf&#95;counter&#40;&#41;<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;result &#61; func&#40;&#42;args, &#42;&#42;kwargs&#41;<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;print&#40;f&#34;&#123;func&#46;&#95;&#95;name&#95;&#95;&#125; took &#123;time&#46;perf&#95;counter&#40;&#41; &#45; start&#58;&#46;4f&#125;s&#34;&#41;<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;return result<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;return wrapper<br><br>&#64;timed<br>def add&#40;a, b&#41;&#58;<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;return a &#43; b<br>&#96;&#96;&#96;<br><br>2&#46; A Python generator is a function that uses &#96;yield&#96; to produce a sequence of values lazily, one at a time, rather than building the entire sequence in memory&#46; Calling a generator function returns a generator object, which is an iterator; each call to &#96;next&#40;&#41;&#96; resumes execution from where it last yielded&#46; This makes generators well suited for processing large files, streams of data, or infinite sequences&#46;<br><br>Because generators hold only the current state rather than the full collection, they use constant memory regardless of how many items they eventually produce&#46; The expression under the square root, &#36;b&#94;2 &#45; 4ac&#36;, is called the discriminant&#58; it is positive when there are two distinct real roots, zero when there is one repeated root, and negative when the roots are complex conjugates&#46; The formula is stated below as Equation &#40;1&#41; so it can be referenced later&#46;<br><br>&#36;&#36;<br>x &#61; &#92;frac&#123;&#45;b &#92;pm &#92;sqrt&#123;b&#94;2 &#45; 4ac&#125;&#125;&#123;2a&#125; A quadratic equation has the general form &#36;ax&#94;2 &#43; bx &#43; c &#61; 0&#36;, where &#36;a&#36;, &#36;b&#36;, and &#36;c&#36; are constants and &#36;a &#92;neq 0&#36;&#46; Rather than factoring by trial and error, we can always find its solutions by completing the square, which yields a closed&#45;form expression that works for any coefficients&#46; This result, known as the quadratic formula, is given in Equation &#40;1&#41; below, and it tells us that the two roots depend entirely on the discriminant &#36;b&#94;2 &#45; 4ac&#36;, whose sign determines whether the roots are real or complex&#46;<br><br>&#36;&#36;<br>x &#61; &#92;frac&#123;&#45;b &#92;pm &#92;sqrt&#123;b&#94;2 &#45; 4ac&#125;&#125;&#123;2a&#125; &#92;tag&#123;1&#125;<br>&#36;&#36;<br><br>As Equation &#40;1&#41; shows, when the discriminant is positive there are two distinct real roots, when it is zero there is exactly one repeated root, and when it is negative the roots form a complex&#45;conjugate pair&#46;</code> |

| 14.1 | Abstract | Write the beginning of a short paper with a title, an author line, and an abstract set apart from the body. | <code>&#35; Adaptive Cache Eviction for Low&#45;Memory Edge Inference<br><br>&#42;&#42;&#91;Author Name&#93;&#42;&#42;<br>&#91;Department, Institution&#93;<br>&#91;email&#64;institution&#46;edu&#93;<br><br>&#45;&#45;&#45;<br><br>&#42;&#42;Abstract&#42;&#42;<br><br>Deploying machine learning models on edge devices is constrained by limited memory, which often forces systems to reload model weights and intermediate results from slower storage&#46; Standard cache eviction policies such as least&#45;recently&#45;used &#40;LRU&#41; and least&#45;frequently&#45;used &#40;LFU&#41; were designed for general&#45;purpose workloads and do not account for the predictable, layered access patterns of neural network inference&#46; We propose a lightweight eviction policy that weights cached tensors by their position in the computation graph and their expected reuse distance&#46; We evaluate the policy on three convolutional and transformer&#45;based models running on a constrained embedded platform&#46; Across these workloads, the proposed policy reduces cache miss rates by up to 31&#37; relative to LRU and reduces end&#45;to&#45;end latency by 12–18&#37;, with negligible overhead in bookkeeping&#46; These results suggest that domain&#45;aware eviction can meaningfully improve inference performance without additional hardware&#46;<br><br>&#45;&#45;&#45;<br><br>&#42;&#42;1&#46; Introduction&#42;&#42;<br><br>Edge computing moves inference closer to the data source, reducing network latency and preserving privacy&#46; However, edge devices typically offer a fraction of the memory available on datacenter accelerators, and many must run several models concurrently&#46; When the working set of tensors exceeds available cache, the runtime must repeatedly evict and reload data, and these transfers often dominate inference time&#46;<br><br>Cache management in most systems relies on general&#45;purpose heuristics&#46; LRU assumes that recently used data is likely to be used again soon, and LFU assumes that frequently used data remains valuable&#46; Neither assumption holds cleanly for neural network inference&#46; A forward pass touches each layer&#39;s weights exactly once in a fixed order, so the most recently used tensor is often the one that will not be needed again until the next inference request&#46; As a result, LRU tends to evict precisely the data that will be needed soonest in the following pass&#46;<br><br>This paper makes three contributions&#46; First, we characterize the access patterns of common inference workloads and show that reuse distance is largely determined by layer position&#46; Second, we introduce a retention score that combines static graph structure with observed timing and use it to guide eviction&#46; Third, we implement the policy in a minimal runtime and evaluate it on representative models&#46; The remainder of the paper is organized as follows&#46; Section 2 reviews related work on caching and memory management for learning systems&#46; Section 3 describes the access model and the proposed policy&#46; Section 4 presents our experimental setup and results, and Section 5 concludes&#46;</code> | <code>&#35; The Quiet Commons&#58; Attention as a Shared Resource in Networked Communities<br><br>&#42;&#42;Joseph Wecker&#42;&#42;<br>&#42;October 15, 2026&#42;<br><br>&#45;&#45;&#45;<br><br>&#62; &#42;&#42;Abstract&#42;&#42;<br>&#62;<br>&#62; Digital platforms increasingly mediate how communities allocate their most limited collective resource&#58; attention&#46; This paper argues that attention is best understood as a commons, subject to the same dynamics of overuse, enclosure, and stewardship that Ostrom and others identified in physical resource systems&#46; We examine how design choices such as notification defaults, ranking algorithms, and the cost of posting shape the sustainability of shared attention in small online communities&#46; Drawing on a comparative reading of existing governance literature, we propose three principles for attention stewardship&#58; bounded visibility, proportional cost, and legible curation&#46; We conclude that communities which treat attention as a finite, jointly held resource tend to sustain higher&#45;quality discourse than those which treat it as an unlimited input to be maximized&#46;<br>&#62;<br>&#62; &#42;&#42;Keywords&#58;&#42;&#42; attention economy, commons governance, online communities, platform design<br><br>&#45;&#45;&#45;<br><br>&#35;&#35; 1&#46; Introduction<br><br>Every community, whether a village, a congregation, or a forum of a few hundred members, depends on a resource that rarely appears in its accounting&#58; the willingness of its members to listen&#46; A town meeting can host only so many speakers before the evening ends&#46; A mailing list can carry only so many threads before readers stop opening them&#46; Attention, in other words, is scarce, and its scarcity is not a defect of any particular platform but a basic condition of collective life&#46;<br><br>Yet much of the contemporary design of networked systems proceeds as though attention were abundant, or as though its consumption were an unambiguous good&#46; Engagement is counted, graphed, and optimized, while the question of who bears the cost of that engagement goes largely unasked&#46; The result is a familiar pattern&#58; contributions multiply, signal thins, and participants withdraw not because they have nothing to say, but because they can no longer be heard, or can no longer bear to listen&#46;<br><br>This paper approaches the problem through the lens of commons governance&#46; Section 2 reviews the relevant literature on shared&#45;resource management&#46; Section 3 develops the claim that attention exhibits the key features of a commons, namely rivalry, difficulty of exclusion, and vulnerability to overuse&#46; Section 4 presents three design principles for stewardship, and Section 5 considers objections and limits&#46;<br><br>&#35;&#35; 2&#46; Background<br><br>The classic framing of shared resources as inevitably doomed to depletion has long since been complicated by empirical work showing that communities frequently devise durable rules for managing what they hold in common&#46;&#46;&#46;</code> |

| 14.3 | Theorem and proof | Write a short mathematical passage with a numbered theorem followed by its proof, ending with an end-of-proof mark. | <code>&#42;&#42;Theorem 1&#46;&#42;&#42; The sum of two even integers is even&#46;<br><br>&#42;&#42;Proof&#46;&#42;&#42; Let &#36;a&#36; and &#36;b&#36; be even integers&#46; By definition, there exist integers &#36;m&#36; and &#36;n&#36; such that &#36;a &#61; 2m&#36; and &#36;b &#61; 2n&#36;&#46; Then<br>&#36;&#36;a &#43; b &#61; 2m &#43; 2n &#61; 2&#40;m &#43; n&#41;&#46;&#36;&#36;<br>Since &#36;m &#43; n&#36; is an integer, &#36;a &#43; b&#36; is twice an integer, and therefore &#36;a &#43; b&#36; is even&#46; &#36;&#92;blacksquare&#36;</code> | <code>&#42;&#42;Theorem 1&#46;&#42;&#42; There are infinitely many prime numbers&#46;<br><br>&#42;Proof&#46;&#42; Suppose, for contradiction, that there are only finitely many primes, say &#36;p&#95;1, p&#95;2, &#92;dots, p&#95;n&#36;&#46; Consider the integer<br><br>&#36;&#36;N &#61; p&#95;1 p&#95;2 &#92;cdots p&#95;n &#43; 1&#46;&#36;&#36;<br><br>Since &#36;N &#62; 1&#36;, it has at least one prime divisor &#36;q&#36;&#46; By assumption, &#36;q&#36; must be one of the &#36;p&#95;i&#36;, so &#36;q&#36; divides the product &#36;p&#95;1 p&#95;2 &#92;cdots p&#95;n&#36;&#46; But &#36;q&#36; also divides &#36;N&#36;, and therefore &#36;q&#36; divides the difference<br><br>&#36;&#36;N &#45; p&#95;1 p&#95;2 &#92;cdots p&#95;n &#61; 1&#46;&#36;&#36;<br><br>This is impossible, since no prime divides &#36;1&#36;&#46; The contradiction shows that the assumption was false, so there are infinitely many primes&#46; &#36;&#92;blacksquare&#36;</code> |
| 14.4 | Numbered definition | Write a numbered definition of a prime number, set apart from the surrounding text the way math books do. | <code>&#42;&#42;Definition 1&#46;1 &#40;Prime number&#41;&#46;&#42;&#42; An integer &#36;p &#62; 1&#36; is called &#42;prime&#42; if its only positive divisors are &#36;1&#36; and &#36;p&#36;&#46; An integer greater than &#36;1&#36; that is not prime is called &#42;composite&#42;&#46;</code> | <code>&#42;&#42;Definition 1&#46;1&#42;&#42; &#40;Prime number&#41;&#46; A natural number &#36;p&#36; is called &#42;prime&#42; if &#36;p &#62; 1&#36; and its only positive divisors are &#36;1&#36; and &#36;p&#36; itself&#46; A natural number greater than &#36;1&#36; that is not prime is called &#42;composite&#42;&#46;<br><br>&#45;&#45;&#45;<br><br>For example, &#36;2, 3, 5, 7, 11&#36; are prime, while &#36;4 &#61; 2 &#92;cdot 2&#36; and &#36;12 &#61; 3 &#92;cdot 4&#36; are composite&#46; Note that &#36;1&#36; is neither prime nor composite&#46;</code> |

| 14.6 | Pull quote | Write a paragraph of an article and a pull quote that repeats a striking line from it in larger type. | <code>&#42;&#42;The Quiet Return of the Paper Map&#42;&#42;<br><br>In an age of turn&#45;by&#45;turn directions, the paper map has quietly become something other than a tool&#46; Unfolding one on a café table means committing to a picture of the whole terrain at once, something no phone screen allows&#46; Its creases hold the memory of every past trip, and its margins hold the pencil marks of people who got lost and found their way back&#46; Navigation apps promise that we will never be lost again, but the paper map suggests that being lost was never the real problem&#46; The real problem was never knowing where we stood in relation to everything else&#46; &#42;&#42;&#34;A map doesn&#39;t tell you where to go&#46; It tells you where you are&#46;&#34;&#42;&#42;<br><br>&#62; &#35; &#34;A map doesn&#39;t tell you where to go&#46; It tells you where you are&#46;&#34;</code> | <code>The town&#39;s last working payphone sits outside a shuttered hardware store on Alder Street, and on most days nobody touches it&#46; But every Thursday around noon, a man in a gray windbreaker arrives, lifts the receiver, and listens&#46; He doesn&#39;t dial&#46; He doesn&#39;t speak&#46; When I asked him why, he looked at me as if the question were slightly rude&#46; &#34;My wife used to call me on this line when I worked the counter,&#34; he said&#46; &#34;The phone company says it&#39;s disconnected, but I figured nobody told the phone&#46;&#34; He hung up gently, the way you&#39;d close a door on a sleeping child, and walked home&#46; In an age when every conversation can be recorded, searched, and archived, there is something almost unbearable about a ritual built on a line that carries nothing at all, and yet it seems to carry exactly what he needs&#46;<br><br>&#45;&#45;&#45;<br><br>&#62; &#35;&#35; &#34;The phone company says it&#39;s disconnected, but I figured nobody told the phone&#46;&#34;</code> |

| 14.8 | Algorithm in pseudocode | Write a short algorithm in numbered pseudocode with the caption 'Binary search'. | <code>&#42;&#42;Binary search&#42;&#42;<br><br>1&#46; Set &#96;low&#96; ← 0 and &#96;high&#96; ← length&#40;array&#41; − 1<br>2&#46; While &#96;low&#96; ≤ &#96;high&#96;, repeat steps 3–7<br>3&#46; Set &#96;mid&#96; ← ⌊&#40;&#96;low&#96; &#43; &#96;high&#96;&#41; / 2⌋<br>4&#46; If array&#91;&#96;mid&#96;&#93; &#61; target, return &#96;mid&#96;<br>5&#46; If array&#91;&#96;mid&#96;&#93; &#60; target, set &#96;low&#96; ← &#96;mid&#96; &#43; 1<br>6&#46; Otherwise, set &#96;high&#96; ← &#96;mid&#96; − 1<br>7&#46; Continue to the next iteration<br>8&#46; Return −1 &#40;target not found&#41;</code> | <code>&#42;&#42;Algorithm 1&#58; Binary search&#42;&#42;<br><br>&#96;&#96;&#96;<br>Input&#58;  A sorted array A&#91;0&#46;&#46;n&#45;1&#93;, target value x<br>Output&#58; Index of x in A, or &#45;1 if not found<br><br>1&#46;  low  ← 0<br>2&#46;  high ← n &#45; 1<br>3&#46;  while low ≤ high do<br>4&#46;      mid ← low &#43; ⌊&#40;high &#45; low&#41; / 2⌋<br>5&#46;      if A&#91;mid&#93; &#61; x then<br>6&#46;          return mid<br>7&#46;      else if A&#91;mid&#93; &#60; x then<br>8&#46;          low ← mid &#43; 1<br>9&#46;      else<br>10&#46;         high ← mid &#45; 1<br>11&#46;     end if<br>12&#46; end while<br>13&#46; return &#45;1<br>&#96;&#96;&#96;<br><br>&#42;&#42;Notes&#58;&#42;&#42;<br>&#45; Runs in O&#40;log n&#41; time and O&#40;1&#41; space&#46;<br>&#45; Line 4 computes the midpoint as &#96;low &#43; ⌊&#40;high &#45; low&#41;/2⌋&#96; to avoid integer overflow&#46;</code> |

| 14.12 | Appendix | Write a short document with two main sections followed by an appendix labeled with a letter. | <code>&#35; Adopting Automated Code Review<br><br>&#35;&#35; Purpose<br><br>This document proposes introducing automated code review into our development workflow&#46; Automated checks can catch common defects, enforce style conventions, and free human reviewers to focus on design and logic&#46; The goal is faster reviews and fewer defects reaching production, without slowing developers down&#46;<br><br>&#35;&#35; Proposed Approach<br><br>Automated review would run on every pull request in three stages&#58;<br><br>1&#46; &#42;&#42;Static analysis&#42;&#42; checks for common bugs, security issues, and dependency vulnerabilities&#46; Findings block merging only for high&#45;severity issues&#46;<br>2&#46; &#42;&#42;Style and formatting&#42;&#42; checks run automatically and are fixed by a bot where possible, so reviewers never comment on whitespace or naming conventions&#46;<br>3&#46; &#42;&#42;Human review&#42;&#42; remains required for every change&#46; Automated tools flag issues but do not approve code&#46;<br><br>We will begin with one team for four weeks, track metrics such as review turnaround time, number of defects found after merge, and developer feedback, and then decide whether to expand&#46;<br><br>&#35;&#35; Static analysis &#124; SonarQube, Semgrep &#124; Finds bugs and security issues across many languages &#124; Can produce false positives that need tuning &#124;<br>&#124; Formatting &#124; Prettier, Black &#124; Eliminates style debates entirely &#124; Limited to style; catches no logic errors &#124;<br>&#124; Dependency scanning &#124; Dependabot, Snyk &#124; Flags known vulnerable packages &#124; Only covers known CVEs &#124;</code> | <code>&#35; Remote Work Guidelines<br><br>&#35;&#35; Section 1&#58; Communication Expectations<br><br>Clear communication keeps distributed teams aligned&#46; Team members should&#58;<br><br>&#45; &#42;&#42;Respond promptly&#46;&#42;&#42; Acknowledge direct messages within four working hours, even if only to say a fuller reply is coming&#46;<br>&#45; &#42;&#42;Default to written updates&#46;&#42;&#42; Share progress in the team channel so decisions and context are visible to everyone, including those in other time zones&#46;<br>&#45; &#42;&#42;Use video selectively&#46;&#42;&#42; Reserve meetings for discussions that benefit from real&#45;time interaction, such as brainstorming, sensitive feedback, or resolving disagreements&#46;<br>&#45; &#42;&#42;State availability&#46;&#42;&#42; Keep calendars and status indicators current so colleagues know when to expect responses&#46;<br><br>&#35;&#35; Section 2&#58; Workspace and Wellbeing<br><br>A sustainable remote setup supports both productivity and health&#46; Team members are encouraged to&#58;<br><br>&#45; &#42;&#42;Set boundaries&#46;&#42;&#42; Define working hours and communicate them to the team&#46; Log off at the end of the day&#46;<br>&#45; &#42;&#42;Take regular breaks&#46;&#42;&#42; Step away from the screen at least once every 90 minutes&#46;<br>&#45; &#42;&#42;Maintain a dedicated space&#46;&#42;&#42; Where possible, work from a consistent, comfortable area with good lighting and ergonomic seating&#46;<br>&#45; | 14.17 | Line numbers for review | Write a short paragraph set up so that the printed version shows line numbers in the margin for reviewers. | <code>Here&#39;s a short LaTeX example using the &#96;lineno&#96; package&#46; It numbers each line in the left margin, and the numbers appear in the printed output for reviewers&#46;<br><br>&#96;&#96;&#96;latex<br>&#92;documentclass&#91;12pt&#93;&#123;article&#125;<br>&#92;usepackage&#91;margin&#61;1in&#93;&#123;geometry&#125;<br>&#92;usepackage&#123;lineno&#125;<br>&#92;linenumbers                 &#37; turn on margin line numbers<br>&#92;modulolinenumbers&#91;5&#93;        &#37; label every fifth line<br>&#92;linenumbersep&#61;8pt           &#37; space between numbers and text<br>&#92;setlength&#123;&#92;linenumbersep&#125;&#123;8pt&#125;<br>&#92;doublespacing               &#37; optional&#58; double spacing gives reviewers room to annotate<br><br>&#92;begin&#123;document&#125;<br><br>&#92;begin&#123;center&#125;<br>&#92;textbf&#123;Manuscript Title&#125;<br>&#92;end&#123;center&#125;<br><br>This paragraph is set up so that the printed version shows line numbers in the left margin&#46; Each line of text is numbered consecutively, which lets reviewers refer to specific passages by line number in their comments&#46; The numbering restarts on each page by default, and it does not count headings, figures, or captions unless they are explicitly included&#46; Because the numbers are generated at typesetting time, they update automatically whenever the text is edited&#46;<br><br>&#92;end&#123;document&#125;<br>&#96;&#96;&#96;<br><br>To make numbering continuous across the whole document, add &#96;&#92;linenumbers&#96; once in the preamble and avoid &#96;&#92;nolinenumbers&#96; around sections you want numbered&#46; If you use a different typesetting system, such as Word, tell me which one and I&#39;ll give you the equivalent setup&#46;</code> | <code>Here&#39;s a minimal LaTeX document using the &#96;lineno&#96; package, which numbers each line in the margin when printed or exported to PDF&#58;<br><br>&#96;&#96;&#96;latex<br>&#92;documentclass&#91;11pt&#93;&#123;article&#125;<br>&#92;usepackage&#91;margin&#61;1in&#93;&#123;geometry&#125;<br>&#92;usepackage&#123;lineno&#125;<br><br>&#92;linenumbers          &#37; turn on line numbering for the whole document<br>&#92;setlength&#123;&#92;linenumbersep&#125;&#123;0&#46;3in&#125;  &#37; gap between numbers and text<br><br>&#92;begin&#123;document&#125;<br><br>Peer review is most effective when reviewers can point to exact locations<br>in a manuscript&#46; Numbering each line in the margin removes ambiguity, so a<br>comment such as &#96;&#96;clarify the claim on line 7&#39;&#39; is immediately actionable&#46;<br>Authors can then resolve feedback quickly, and editors can track which<br>passages drew the most discussion across multiple reviewers&#46;<br><br>&#92;end&#123;document&#125;<br>&#96;&#96;&#96;<br><br>&#42;&#42;Notes&#42;&#42;<br><br>&#45; Compile with &#96;pdflatex&#96; &#40;or &#96;xelatex&#96;/&#96;lualatex&#96;&#41;; line numbers appear in the left margin of the PDF&#46;<br>&#45; Use &#96;&#92;linenumbers&#91;1&#93;&#96; to restart numbering at 1, or &#96;&#92;begin&#123;linenumbers&#125;&#46;&#46;&#46;&#92;end&#123;linenumbers&#125;&#96; to number only a section&#46;<br>&#45; Add &#96;&#92;modulolinenumbers&#91;5&#93;&#96; if you&#39;d prefer a number only every 5th line&#46;<br>&#45; With &#96;amsmath&#96; equations, load &#96;lineno&#96; with the &#96;&#91;mathlines&#93;&#96; option &#40;&#96;&#92;usepackage&#91;mathlines&#93;&#123;lineno&#125;&#96;&#41; to avoid numbering glitches&#46;<br><br>If you&#39;d rather use Word or Google Docs, let me know and I can give the equivalent steps &#40;e&#46;g&#46;, Layout → Line Numbers → Continuous in Word&#41;&#46;</code> |
